Update running max m_i = max(m_{i-1}, max(S)) Update running sum l_i = exp(m_{i-1} - m_i) * l_{i-1} + sum(exp(S - m_i)) Then update O: O_i = (exp(m_{i-1} - m_i) * O_{i-1} + P_i @ V) / l_i * l_i? Is that okay for WMMA? `load_matrix_sync` requires the leading dimension to be >= 16 and for row_major, contiguous elements in a row. There might be alignment requirements for the base pointer. Each row of Ps starts at `w*64*72 + r*72` bf16. Address = base + (w*64*72 + r*72)*2. We need this to be 128-bit aligned for float4/WMMA loads. 72*2=144. 144 is divisible by 16? 144/16=9. Yes. Warp w base = base + w*64*72*2. 64*72*2 = 9216. Divisible by 16 (9216/16=576). Yes. So `Ps[4][64][72]` with ld=72 works. Similarly Qs: `Qs[4][64][136]`. ld=136. 136*2=272. 272/16=17. Address per warp: w*64*136*2 = w*17408. 17408/16 = 1088. Divisible. Good. Ks/Vs: ld=136. Great. The shared memory layout works. Now let's detail the steps in the kernel: ```cuda __global__ void mha_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ Q, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ K, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ V, __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ O, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, int D, float scale ) { // Grid: (N/Br, Hkv, B) // Block: 128 threads = 4 warps int warp_id = threadIdx.x / 32; int lane = threadIdx.x % 32; int b = blockIdx.z; int kv_head = blockIdx.y; int q_head = kv_head * (Hq/Hkv) + warp_id; // 4 warps per 4 query heads int seq_tile = blockIdx.x; int q_row = seq_tile * Br; // Br=64 // Pointers to Q, K, V, O for this head/tile const int q_stride_B = Hq * N * D; const int q_stride_H = N * D; const int kv_stride_B = Hkv * N * D; const int kv_stride_H = N * D; // Q pointer for this warp's head const __nv_bfloat16* q_ptr = Q + b * q_stride_B + q_head * q_stride_H + q_row * D; const __nv_bfloat16* k_ptr = K + b * kv_stride_B + kv_head * kv_stride_H; // iterate later const __nv_bfloat16* v_ptr = V + b * kv_stride_B + kv_head * kv_stride_H; __nv_bfloat16* o_ptr = O + b * q_stride_B + q_head * q_stride_H + q_row * D; // Shared memory layout __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 Qs[4][Br][D_PAD]; // D_PAD=136 __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 Ks[Bc][D_PAD]; __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 Vs[Bc][D_PAD]; __shared__ float Ss[4][Br][Bc]; // 64 rows per warp, 64 cols __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 Ps[4][Br][Bc_PAD]; // Bc_PAD=72 // Each warp loads its own Q tile // 64 rows * 128 cols = 8192 bf16 per head. 32 threads -> 256 bf16 per thread. // Using float4 (8 bf16): 32 loads per thread. for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Br * D_PAD / 8; i += blockDim.x) { // careful: only load D elements, not D_PAD // But using float4, we load 8 at a time. // We might overshoot D. D=128, D_PAD=136. 128 is multiple of 8. int row = i / (D_PAD / 8); int col = (i % (D_PAD / 8)) * 8; if (row < Br && col < D) { // compute global memory address // q_ptr is warp-specific int q_idx = row * D + col; // careful with alignment: q_ptr + q_idx must be 16-byte aligned. // q_ptr points to q_row * D. q_row is multiple of Br. D=128. // row*D + col: col is multiple of 8. D is multiple of 8. So aligned. float4 val = *((const float4*)(q_ptr + q_idx)); int s_idx = warp_id * Br * D_PAD + row * D_PAD + col; *((float4*)(&Qs[0][0][0] + s_idx)) = val; } } __syncthreads(); // Initialize m and l for this warp float m_reg[Br]; // too many registers? 64 floats = 256 bytes per thread. 4 warps -> 1KB per thread? No, per thread! // 64 floats per thread is too much. We only need per row. Each warp handles 64 rows. // We can store m and l in shared memory: Ss_m[4][Br] and Ss_l[4][Br]. // Or compute them cooperatively in registers: only 16 values if we process 16 rows per step. ``` Wait, if a warp handles Br=64 rows, and we need m and l for each row, that's 64 values. Per warp, storing in shared memory is fine: `__shared__ float m_shared[4][Br];` `__shared__ float l_shared[4][Br];` Initialize by warp: warp w writes m_shared[w][r] = -INFINITY, l_shared[w][r] = 0 for r=0..63. Each warp can initialize its own rows using 32 threads: each thread initializes 2 rows. Or just have thread 0 loop and write. Simpler: each warp writes independently after syncthreads? Warps are in the same block. We can just have all threads initialize: ```cuda for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < 4*Br; i += blockDim.x) { m_shared[i/Br][i%Br] = -INFINITY; l_shared[i/Br][i%Br] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); ``` Now the KV loop: ```cuda for (int tile = 0; tile < N / Bc; ++tile) { int kv_row = tile * Bc; // Load Ks and Vs // 128 threads load 64*128 = 8192 bf16 = 16KB. Each thread loads 128 bytes = 8 float4. const __nv_bfloat16* k_tile_ptr = k_ptr + kv_row * D; const __nv_bfloat16* v_tile_ptr = v_ptr + kv_row * D; for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Bc * D_PAD / 8; i += blockDim.x) { int row = i / (D_PAD / 8); int col = (i % (D_PAD / 8)) * 8; if (row < Bc && col < D) { int kv_idx = row * D + col; float4 kval = *((const float4*)(k_tile_ptr + kv_idx)); float4 vval = *((const float4*)(v_tile_ptr + kv_idx)); int s_idx = row * D_PAD + col; *((float4*)(&Ks[0][0] + s_idx)) = kval; *((float4*)(&Vs[0][0] + s_idx)) = vval; } } __syncthreads(); // QK: each warp computes Ss[warp_id] = Qs[warp_id] @ Ks^T // Ss is 64x64 float. // Zero Ss for this warp first. for (int i = lane; i < Br * Bc; i += 32) { int r = i / Bc; int c = i % Bc; Ss[warp_id][r][c] = 0.0f; } // WMMA for QK wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, 16, 16, 16, __nv_bfloat16, wmma::row_major> q_frag; wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, 16, 16, 16, __nv_bfloat16, wmma::col_major> k_frag; wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, 16, 16, 16, float> s_frag; // Each warp handles 1 row tile in Q (rows 0..63) and all 4 col tiles in K (cols 0..63) // Actually Br=64 -> 4 row tiles of 16. Bc=64 -> 4 col tiles of 16. // Each warp computes all 4x4 = 16 tiles? Wait. // Warp handles all 64 rows and all 64 cols. That's 4x4 = 16 tiles. // 16 tiles * 8 K steps = 128 mma per warp. At low occupancy maybe okay. // Each tile is 16x16x16. 128 mma. Per KV tile (32 total). 4096 mma per warp total. // H100 can do a lot, but let's see if latency is hidden. for (int tile_r = 0; tile_r < Br/16; ++tile_r) { for (int tile_c = 0; tile_c < Bc/16; ++tile_c) { wmma::fill_fragment(s_frag, 0.0f); for (int k = 0; k < D/16; ++k) { // load q_frag from Qs[warp_id][tile_r*16 .. ][k*16 ..] int q_offset = warp_id * Br * D_PAD + (tile_r*16) * D_PAD + k*16; wmma::load_matrix_sync(q_frag, &Qs[0][0][0] + q_offset, D_PAD); // load k_frag from Ks[tile_c*16 .. ][k*16 ..] int k_offset = (tile_c*16) * D_PAD + k*16; wmma::load_matrix_sync(k_frag, &Ks[0][0][0] + k_offset, D_PAD); wmma::mma_sync(s_frag, q_frag, k_frag, s_frag); } // store s_frag to Ss int s_offset = warp_id * Br * Bc + (tile_r*16) * Bc + tile_c*16; wmma::store_matrix_sync(&Ss[0][0][0] + s_offset, s_frag, Bc, wmma::mem_row_major); } } __syncthreads(); // Wait for all warps to finish QK // Softmax for this warp // For each row r in 0..Br-1: // old_m = m_shared[warp_id][r] // local max over c in 0..Bc-1 // We can have all threads in warp participate for each row, but 32 threads for 64 elements. // Process 2 rows in parallel? Or each thread handles 2 elements per row. // Let's do it simply: for each row r (0..Br-1): // float local_max = -INFINITY; // for (int c = lane; c < Bc; c += 32) local_max = fmaxf(local_max, Ss[warp_id][r][c]); // local_max = warp_reduce_max(local_max); // float new_m = fmaxf(old_m, local_max); // float exp_old = expf(old_m - new_m); // float sum_exp = 0.0f; // for (int c = lane; c < Bc; c += 32) { // float p = expf(Ss[warp_id][r][c] * scale - new_m); // apply scale here! // Ss[warp_id][r][c] = p; // save temporarily? Or write to Ps directly. // sum_exp += p; // } // sum_exp = warp_reduce_sum(sum_exp); // l_shared[warp_id][r] = exp_old * l_shared[warp_id][r] + sum_exp; // m_shared[warp_id][r] = new_m; // // Now write p to Ps // for (int c = lane; c < Bc; c += 32) { // Ps[warp_id][r][c] = __float2bfloat16(Ss[warp_id][r][c]); // p saved above // } // Wait, we need to apply causal mask? No, is_causal=false. // Apply scale: QK^T * sm_scale. // In QK compute, we didn't apply scale. So do it here: Ss[w][r][c] *= scale. // Actually in the loop above, we can compute p = expf(Ss[w][r][c] * scale - new_m); // But writing back to Ss then reading again for Ps is extra work. We can write directly to Ps inside the loop. // But Ps is bf16. So we compute p, accumulate sum_exp, write __float2bfloat16(p) to Ps[w][r][c]. // However, we need `p` for sum_exp. So: // p = expf(Ss[w][r][c] * scale - new_m); // Ps[w][r][c] = __float2bfloat16(p); // sum_exp += p; // // l_new = exp_old * l_old + sum_exp; (after warp reduce) // This works! // Important: We also need to scale the O accumulator BEFORE adding the new PV contribution. // exp_old = expf(old_m - new_m) // This is the same for all columns in the row. // We can apply this scaling when we do the O = ... + P @ V later. // Or we can scale O_frag now. So offset in bytes is 4 * multiple_of_16. Wait, offset is in elements. `Ss[r][c]` is float. `&Ss[0][0]` is `float*`. Adding `N` gives `float* + N floats`. The byte offset is `N*4`. If N is multiple of 4, offset is 16-byte aligned. // `(warp_id*16)*64 + tile_c*16`. warp_id*16*64 = warp_id*1024. Divisible by 4. tile_c*16 divisible by 4. So yes, 16-byte aligned. // For Os: `&Os[0][0] + (warp_id*16)*D_PAD + tile_d*16`. D_PAD=136. 136*16=2176. Divisible by 4 -> 16-byte aligned. // So alignment is fine. // Let me carefully code the kernel. // One performance note: in the QK phase, each warp iterates over tile_r and tile_c. With 4 warps, each does 4 row tiles x 4 col tiles = 16 tiles. Each tile does 8 k-steps with mma. That's 128 mma per warp per KV tile. With 32 KV tiles, 4096 mma. Plus PV: each warp does 4 row tiles x 8 col tiles = 32 tiles, each with 4 k-steps (Bc/16=4). 128 mma per warp per KV tile. Total 256 mma per warp per KV tile. 32 iterations -> 8192 mma per warp. // Each mma is for 16x16x16. An H100 SM can do many per cycle. With 4 warps per block and 16 blocks per SM -> 64 warps per SM. This should keep the SM busy. // Let's write the code. I'll be very meticulous. // Wait, there's a synchronization issue: In the QK phase, all warps write to `Ss` (block-global). They write disjoint row tiles. After all warps finish, `__syncthreads()` ensures Ss is ready. // Then in softmax, each warp reads Ss, writes Ks (disjoint rows). After `__syncthreads()`, Ks is ready. // Then in PV, all warps read Ks and Vs and write Os (disjoint rows). After `__syncthreads()`, Os is ready for scaling in next iteration. // This is correct. // Let's code step by step. ```cuda #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cuda_bf16.h> #include <mma.h> #include <cmath> using namespace nvcuda; #define Br 64 #define Bc 64 #define D 128 #define D_PAD 136 #define Bc_PAD 64 // since we use Ks directly, and its second dim is D_PAD, for Ss __device__ inline float warpReduceMax(float val) { for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) val = fmaxf(val, __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset)); return val; } __device__ inline float warpReduceSum(float val) { for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); return val; } __global__ void mha_fwd_kernel( const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ Q, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ K, const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ V, __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ O, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, int D_real, float scale ) { // blockIdx.x: seq tile (0..N/Br-1) // blockIdx.y: head_q (0..Hq-1) // blockIdx.z: batch (0..B-1) int b = blockIdx.z; int hq = blockIdx.y; int seq_tile = blockIdx.x; int group_size = Hq / Hkv; int hkv = hq / group_size; int q_row = seq_tile * Br; int warp_id = threadIdx.x / 32; int lane = threadIdx.x % 32; // Strides (assuming row-major B,H,N,D) int stride_q_b = Hq * N * D_real; int stride_q_h = N * D_real; int stride_kv_b = Hkv * N * D_real; int stride_kv_h = N * D_real; const __nv_bfloat16* q_ptr = Q + b * stride_q_b + hq * stride_q_h + q_row * D_real; const __nv_bfloat16* k_base = K + b * stride_kv_b + hkv * stride_kv_h; const __nv_bfloat16* v_base = V + b * stride_kv_b + hkv * stride_kv_h; __nv_bfloat16* o_ptr = O + b * stride_q_b + hq * stride_q_h + q_row * D_real; // Shared memory __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 Qs[Br][D_PAD]; __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 Ks[Bc][D_PAD]; __shared__ __nv_bfloat16 Vs[Bc][D_PAD]; __shared__ float Ss[Br][Bc]; __shared__ float Os[Br][D_PAD]; __shared__ float m_shared[Br]; __shared__ float l_shared[Br]; // Zero Os, m, l for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Br * D_PAD; i += blockDim.x) { int r = i / D_PAD; int c = i % D_PAD; if (c < D_real) Os[r][c] = 0.0f; } for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Br; i += blockDim.x) { m_shared[i] = -INFINITY; l_shared[i] = 0.0f; } __syncthreads(); // Load Q tile // Each thread loads several float4 for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Br * D_real / 8; i += blockDim.x) { int row = i / (D_real / 8); int col = (i % (D_real / 8)) * 8; float4 val = ((const float4*)(q_ptr + row * D_real + col))[0]; int srow = row; int scol = col; ((float4*)(&Qs[srow][scol]))[0] = val; } __syncthreads(); // Loop over KV tiles int num_kv_tiles = N / Bc; for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < num_kv_tiles; ++kv_tile) { int kv_row = kv_tile * Bc; const __nv_bfloat16* k_ptr = k_base + kv_row * D_real; const __nv_bfloat16* v_ptr = v_base + kv_row * D_real; // Load K and V tiles for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Bc * D_real / 8; i += blockDim.x) { int row = i / (D_real / 8); int col = (i % (D_real / 8)) * 8; float4 kval = ((const float4*)(k_ptr + row * D_real + col))[0]; float4 vval = ((const float4*)(v_ptr + row * D_real + col))[0]; ((float4*)(&Ks[row][col]))[0] = kval; ((float4*)(&Vs[row][col]))[0] = vval; } __syncthreads(); // QK: Ss = Qs @ Ks^T // Each warp handles one row tile of 16 rows wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, 16, 16, 16, __nv_bfloat16, wmma::row_major> q_frag; wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, 16, 16, 16, __nv_bfloat16, wmma::col_major> k_frag; wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, 16, 16, 16, float> s_frag; for (int tile_r = 0; tile_r < Br/16; ++tile_r) { // Only the warp that owns this row tile? No, all 4 warps can cooperate on all tiles? // If we let each warp do all row tiles, they'd conflict on Ss. // Better: warp w handles tile_r = warp_id. tile_r = warp_id; // Reassign: each warp does exactly one row tile. // Wait, I wrote a loop over tile_r. Let's just have each warp handle its row tile. break; // from loop - actually let's restructure. } // Since each warp does exactly one row tile (16 rows), and all 4 col tiles: int my_tile_r = warp_id; for (int tile_c = 0; tile_c < Bc/16; ++tile_c) { wmma::fill_fragment(s_frag, 0.0f); for (int k = 0; k < D/16; ++k) { int q_offset = (my_tile_r * 16) * D_PAD + k * 16; wmma::load_matrix_sync(q_frag, &Qs[0][0] + q_offset, D_PAD); int k_offset = (tile_c * 16) * D_PAD + k * 16; wmma::load_matrix_sync(k_frag, &Ks[0][0] + k_offset, D_PAD); wmma::mma_sync(s_frag, q_frag, k_frag, s_frag); } int s_offset = (my_tile_r * 16) * Bc + tile_c * 16; wmma::store_matrix_sync(&Ss[0][0] + s_offset, s_frag, Bc, wmma::mem_row_major); } __syncthreads(); // Softmax for this warp's rows // my_tile_r = warp_id (0..3), rows = my_tile_r*16 .. my_tile_r*16+15 for (int rr = 0; rr < 16; ++rr) { int r = my_tile_r * 16 + rr; // max float local_max = -INFINITY; #pragma unroll for (int c = lane; c < Bc; c += 32) { local_max = fmaxf(local_max, Ss[r][c]); } local_max = warpReduceMax(local_max); float old_m = m_shared[r]; float m_new = fmaxf(old_m, local_max); float scale_factor = expf(old_m - m_new); // compute P and sum float local_sum = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int c = lane; c < Bc; c += 32) { float p = expf(Ss[r][c] * scale - m_new); Ks[r][c] = __float2bfloat16_rn(p); local_sum += p; } local_sum = warpReduceSum(local_sum); if (lane == 0) { l_shared[r] = l_shared[r] * scale_factor + local_sum; m_shared[r] = m_new; } // Scale Os row by scale_factor #pragma unroll for (int c = lane; c < D_real; c += 32) { Os[r][c] *= scale_factor; } } __syncthreads(); // PV: Os += P @ V // P is in Ks (bf16, shape Br x Bc). V is in Vs (bf16, shape Bc x D). // Each warp handles its row tile and iterates over D tiles. wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, 16, 16, 16, __nv_bfloat16, wmma::row_major> p_frag; wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, 16, 16, 16, __nv_bfloat16, wmma::col_major> v_frag; wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, 16, 16, 16, float> o_frag; for (int tile_d = 0; tile_d < D_real/16; ++tile_d) { int o_offset = (my_tile_r * 16) * D_PAD + tile_d * 16; wmma::load_matrix_sync(o_frag, &Os[0][0] + o_offset, D_PAD, wmma::mem_row_major); for (int tile_c = 0; tile_c < Bc/16; ++tile_c) { int p_offset = (my_tile_r * 16) * D_PAD + tile_c * 16; wmma::load_matrix_sync(p_frag, &Ks[0][0] + p_offset, D_PAD); int v_offset = (tile_c * 16) * D_PAD + tile_d * 16; wmma::load_matrix_sync(v_frag, &Vs[0][0] + v_offset, D_PAD); wmma::mma_sync(o_frag, p_frag, v_frag, o_frag); } wmma::store_matrix_sync(&Os[0][0] + o_offset, o_frag, D_PAD, wmma::mem_row_major); } __syncthreads(); } // Normalize and write O for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Br; i += blockDim.x) { // Actually loop over all elements } for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < Br * D_real; i += blockDim.x) { int r = i / D_real; int c = i % D_real; float val = Os[r][c] / l_shared[r]; o_ptr[r * D_real + c] = __float2bfloat16_rn(val); } } ``` // Issues with the draft: // 1. offset > 0; offset /= 2)\n val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset);\n return val;\n}\n\n__global__ void mha_fwd_kernel(\n const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ Q,\n const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ K,\n const __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ V,\n __nv_bfloat16* __restrict__ O,\n int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, int D_real, float scale\n) {\n int b = blockIdx.z;\n int hq = blockIdx.y;\n int seq_tile = blockIdx.x;\n int group_size = Hq / Hkv;\n int hkv = hq / group_size;\n int q_row = seq_tile * Br;\n\n int warp_id = threadIdx.x / 32;\n int lane = threadIdx.x % 32;\n\n int stride_q_b = Hq * N * D_real;\n int stride_q_h = N * D_real;\n int stride_kv_b = Hkv * N * D_real;\n int stride_kv_h = N * D_real;\n\n const __nv_bfloat16* q_ptr = Q + b * stride_q_b + hq * stride_q_h + q_row * D_real;\n const __nv_bfloat16* k_base = K + b * stride_kv_b + hkv * stride_kv_h;\n const __nv_bfloat16* v_base = V + b * stride_kv_b + hkv * stride_kv_h;\n __nv_bfloat16* o_ptr = O + b * stride_q_b + hq * stride_q_h + q_row * D_real;\n\n __shared__ __align__(16) __nv_bfloat16 Qs[Br][D_PAD];\n __shared__ __align__(16) __nv_bfloat16 Ks[Bc][D_PAD];\n __shared__ __align__(16) __nv_bfloat16 Vs[Bc][D_PAD];\n __shared__ __align__(16) float Ss[Br][Bc];\n __shared__ __align__(16) float Os[Br][D_PAD];\n __shared__ float m_shared[Br];\n __shared__ float l_shared[Br];\n\n // Zero Os, init m and l\n for (int i = threadIdx.x; tile_c < Bc / 16; ++tile_c) {\n wmma::fill_fragment(s_frag, 0.0f);\n #pragma unroll\n for (int k = 0; k < D / 16; ++k) {\n int q_off = (my_tile_r * 16) * D_PAD + k * 16;\n wmma::load_matrix_sync(q_frag, &Qs[0][0] + q_off, D_PAD);\n int k_off = (tile_c * 16) * D_PAD + k * 16;\n wmma::load_matrix_sync(k_frag, &Ks[0][0] + k_off, D_PAD);\n wmma::mma_sync(s_frag, q_frag, k_frag, s_frag);\n }\n int s_off = (my_tile_r * 16) * Bc + tile_c * 16;\n wmma::store_matrix_sync(&Ss[0][0] + s_off, s_frag, Bc, wmma::mem_row_major);\n }\n __syncthreads();\n\n // Softmax + write P to Ks + scale Os\n for (int rr = 0; rr < 16; ++rr) {\n int r = my_tile_r * 16 + rr;\n float local_max = -INFINITY;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int c = lane; c < Bc; c += 32) {\n local_max = fmaxf(local_max, Ss[r][c]);\n }\n local_max = warp_reduce_max(local_max);\n float old_m = m_shared[r];\n float m_new = fmaxf(old_m, local_max);\n float scale_factor = expf(old_m - m_new);\n\n float local_sum = 0.0f;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int c = lane; For wmma::store_matrix_sync with row_major and ld=64: Thread t in the warp writes specific elements. In typical wmma implementations, threads cooperate to store a tile. The exact mapping depends on the fragment layout. For float accumulator 16x16 tile with row_major store and ld=64: Elements are stored as C[i][j] at offset i*64 + j from base. Address = base + i*64*4 + j*4 = base + i*256 + j*4. Bank = ((base + i*256 + j*4) / 4) % 32 = (base/4 + i*64 + j) % 32. For j in [0, 15], and i varying across threads. Bank conflicts depend on the thread-to-element mapping within wmma. I can't fully control this, but wmma is designed to minimize bank conflicts. Let's trust it for now. Similarly for Qs and Ks (bf16): each element is 2 bytes. With row of 128 elements = 256 bytes. Using float4 load/store which is 16 bytes. The bank size for 2-byte elements: on Hopper, shared memory supports configurable bank size (4 bytes or 8 bytes). Default is 4 bytes. But float4 loads should be fine since they span all banks. Actually, for Qs: float4 load at Qs[row][col*8] where col is multiple of 8. Each float4 spans 16 bytes. With bank size 4 bytes, this covers 4 consecutive banks. For consecutive rows, the offset difference is 256 bytes = 8 * 32 bytes, so row i and row i+1 access banks offset by 8, meaning no conflicts for threads loading from different rows. Let me proceed with the kernel. Another thing: I should use static shared memory allocation instead of dynamic to avoid passing size arguments and potential alignment issues. But I'll just use `__shared__ __align__(16) __nv_bfloat16 Qs[Br][D];` where D=128. But I need D as a compile-time constant. Since this kernel is specifically for head_dim=128, I can use literal 128. Wait, the function parameter has `int D` which is 128. But in the kernel, I can use `constexpr int kHeadDim = 128;` and static arrays based on that. Since this kernel is compiled specifically for hdim=128, it's fine. Let me write the code now, carefully avoiding the `D` macro issue by not using `#define D`. Actually, let me use the simplest approach - request more shared memory via cudaFuncSetAttribute. H100 supports 227KB per block.
assistantThe shared memory exceeds the default 48KB limit. Let me request more shared memory and also optimize the layout

reasoning
The naive approach of just requesting dynamic shared memory via cudaFuncSetAttribute doesn't seem to be working (still seeing 0xc000 = 48KB max). This could be because the environment limits shared memory or nvcc options are overriding. Let me drastically reduce Br to 32 to reduce shared memory usage, or completely rethink the shared memory layout. Actually, looking more carefully, the SM limit is 164KB on H100, but the 48KB limit per block is a CUDA default unless you use dynamic shared memory. But I used cudaFuncSetAttribute in the host code - the problem might be that the static shared variable itself is too big for the compiler's default limit. A better approach: use dynamic shared memory with `extern __shared__`, or reduce Br significantly. Let me reduce Br to 32. This means we only compute 32 query rows per block. With 4 warps and each handling 8 rows, we can still parallelize well. With Br=32, Bc=32: Qs: 32 * 128 * 2 = 8K Ks: 32 * 128 * 2 = 8K Vs: 32 * 128 * 2 = 8K Ss: 32 * 32 * 4 = 4K Os: 32 * 128 * 4 = 16K m/l: 32 * 4 * 2 = 0.5K Total: ~44.5K < 48K That should compile. But Br=32 means more blocks: 2048/32 = 64 blocks per head. Total blocks = 4 * 32 * 64 = 8192, which is plenty to saturate the GPU. But each block does less work and the softmax sequential load on Ss is proportionally smaller. Actually on Hopper, smaller blocks with more parallelism might be OK. But we also have fewer warps per block (2 warps for 32 rows) if we map 1 warp to 16 rows. Total threads = 64. Let me go with Br=32, Bc=32, block size=128 (4 warps, each warp handles 8 rows for accumulation, but we have 32 rows total so 4 warp tiles of 8? WMMA works on 16x16... so we'd need each warp to handle 2 tiles of 16 rows, or use a different tile size. Let me think about this differently. Since WMMA is 16x16, if Br=32, we need 2 tiles of 16 rows. With 4 warps, we could have each warp handle half a tile? No, warp-level MMA needs all 32 threads. Instead, let me use Br=64 but drastically optimize shared memory: - Remove the separate Ss float array. Since Ks is only needed during QK and then during PV we only need the P matrix (in Ks slot), we can compute Ss directly into Ks (or a smaller buffer). Actually after QK, we overwrite Ks with softmax(P). Ss is the intermediate QK result. - Could use a single 64x64 union for Ss and the softmax result, stored as float then cast to bf16 in Ks. Let me remove Ss entirely and do QK -> store directly into a float scratchpad that I immediately process, then write bf16 back. But the real problem is the OSes and Ss float arrays. Os as float[64][128] = 32KB. That's already a big chunk. What if Os is kept in registers frag across tiles? No, because we need to accumulate across all KV tiles. Alternative: use Br=32, Bc=32, and block dim = 128. Br=32: Qs = 8K, Ks = 8K, Vs = 8K Ss = 32*32*4 = 4K Os = 32*128*4 = 16K Total = 44K OK let me go with Br=32. For 4 warps, each warp handles 1 tile of 16 rows. But we have 32 rows total, so we need 2 warps per 16-row tile? Wait, each warp does its own 16x16 MMA. If we have 32 rows, that's 2 row tiles of 16 each. With 4 warps, warps 0 and 1 share the first 16 rows (processing 2 different column tiles simultaneously?). With Bc=32, there are 2 column tiles of 16 each. So warp 0 does row_tile 0 + col_tile 0, warp 1 does row_tile 0 + col_tile 1, warp 2 does row_tile 1 + col_tile 0, warp 3 does row_tile 1 + col_tile 1. That works perfectly! Then for softmax, warp 0 handles rows 0-15, warp 1 handles rows 0-15... wait, warps 0 and 1 both work on rows 0-15 for MMA. For softmax, we can pair warps: warps 0 and 1 both reduce rows 0-15. Actually softmax is element-wise per row, so any thread can do it. With 2 warps working on the same 16 rows, they can split the columns. That's 64 threads per 16 rows, or 4 threads per row. That should be enough. Then for PV, the same 4-warp assignment works: warp 0 does row_tile 0, col accumulation across Bc=32. For each output column tile (there are 128/16=8), we iterate. Warp 0 does row 0-15, col 0-15, col 16-31, etc. This is getting complex. Let me just use Br=32, Bc=32, 4 warps, with warp_id modulo 2 for row tile and warp_id / 2 for... it works. Actually let me simplify by using Br=32, assigning each warp to one 16-row tile, but that needs only 2 warps. What do the other 2 warps do? Idle? Or maybe I should just use blockDim=64. With blockDim=64 (2 warps), each warp handles one 16-row tile: - Warp 0: rows 0-15 - Warp 1: rows 16-31 For QK with Bc=32, each warp iterates through 2 column tiles of 16. That works. For softmax, each warp processes its own 16 rows. For PV, same assignment. This gives 64 threads per block = 2 warps. Total blocks = 4 * 32 * (2048/32) = 8192 blocks. Let me go with this simpler design: Br=32, Bc=32, threads=64.
